Mary Miss’s Long Fight to Keep Public Art Alive

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the early decades of postwar America, public art tended to be seen as the pallid stepchild of architecture and design. Steel, bronze, or stone abstractions sited in front of modernist buildings were denigrated as “plop art,” or even more damningly as “the turd in the plaza.” And, accordingly, public artists were relegated to the province of decoration or historical commemoration while public responses to their art ranged from indifference to outrage over the expenditure of public money.

Then, in the 1980s, public art was jolted by a revolution. Rebelling against the tradition of the static monument, a group of young artists aspired instead to explore how art in public spaces could encourage connection, reflection, and interaction among viewers who didn’t necessarily have an art education. They created mazes, placed mirrors in the landscape, and drew lines in the snow. An extension of the boundary-breaking experiments of avant-garde artists of the 1970s, this new public art slowly began to gain acceptance from funders, developers, government officials, and general audiences. Related Articles Walid Raad's Way of Working Is Inspired by the Mirrors in Vampire Movies Art in America Celebrates Fall "Icons" Issue with Talk Featuring William T. Williams and Nari Ward

Among the most adventurous of these pioneers was Mary Miss. Raised in the American West, she was interested in the tensions between rural and urban landscapes, the built and unbuilt environment, and the ways in which people navigate their path through the spaces of the modern world. By 1988, she had created an impressive roster of temporary and permanent projects that challenged the conventional definitions of public art. That year saw the creation of one of her most celebrated works, a waterside park titled South Cove (1984–87) in the newly created Battery Park City development at the bottommost tip of Manhattan. Created on a former landfill site, this work incorporates natural plantings, rocks, walkways, trellises, wooden pilings, a circular jetty, and an overlook that cantilevers over the edge of the Hudson River. Brilliantly weaving together architecture, landscape, and references to Manhattan’s maritime history, it remains a much-loved intervention in the urban landscape.

The year she made South Cove, Miss sat down with curator Patterson Sims at the 92nd Street Y for a wide-ranging discussion of her work and her views on public art. When he prodded her to speculate on the future of this new way of creating art for the public, Miss replied, “I think we’re just beginning to see what the possibilities are.” Then, reflecting on the support provided for such projects by the National Endowment for the Arts, she added, “I think they’ve started something that can’t stop.”

The irony of this faith in the NEA is not lost on Miss today. This spring, sitting in the Tribeca studio she has occupied since 1970, she recalled the enthusiasm of her younger self with what seemed like envy. “I was still feeling so optimistic,” she says. “I hadn’t been beaten down yet.”

Now a seasoned practitioner, her dedication to public art and dialogue remains intact, but has been tempered by the realities of politics, economics, and societal change. She has endured bureaucratic meddling; watched lofty rhetoric butt up against budgets, architects, and physical and technical limitations; experienced the shrinking and politicization of public money; and seen one of her signature pieces dismantled. In the course of her long career, she’s worked on numerous proposals that haven’t come to fruition, and at times put up with nonpayment and the theft of her ideas.

Yet, while these conditions might have deterred a less determined artist, Miss has responded by adapting: She shifts gears, rethinks her basic premises, and finds a new way. Her current project, “City as Living Laboratory” (CaLL), takes the idea of public art away from its dependence on gatekeepers and experts and returns it to the communities in which it’s created—a testament to her resilience and her remarkable capacity for reinvention.

IT TAKES A SPECIAL kind of person to operate successfully—and continually—as a public artist. Miss was born in New York City in 1944, but spent a peripatetic childhood moving around the country, with extended stays in Colorado and Florida as the family followed her father, who was in the military. She recalls a formative childhood memory of sitting in the back of an old Chevy and watching the scenery spin by. Fences laid across the fields whizzed by in a fine filigree and then gradually disappeared as the urban landscape took over. Such experiences sparked an enduring interest in vernacular forms and quotidian materials. They also provided a metaphor she has frequently invoked of public art as a wide net that stretches across barriers and connects people.

After getting a BA from the University of California, Santa Barbara, Miss came East and enrolled in graduate school at the Maryland Institute College of Art in Baltimore. However, she did not find the faculty very receptive to her interests, so she spent a year out of residence in Colorado. There, she did outdoor projects that contained the germ of her subsequent public work before returning East to New York City in 1968. It was an exciting time to be an artist: While the city itself was struggling economically, cheap rents made it a paradise for the era’s avant-garde. Miss fell in with a group of young women, among them Hannah Wilke, Elizabeth Murray, Nancy Holt, and Alice Aycock, who were challenging the orthodoxies of a male-dominated art world that was still fanning the flames of formalism. Miss recalls, “We figured, OK, you guys aren’t going to play with us. We’ll figure out another way.”

Miss’s early works, extending ideas of the ordinary yet defamiliarized spaces she had begun to explore in Colorado, were constructions using common materials that challenged viewers’ perceptions and sense of space. Some were indoor sculptures comprising bits of rope, ladders, stairs, or barbed wire. Others, using similar materials, were set outside in open fields and on hillsides. She generally eschewed urban contexts, and her modest approach was a marked contrast to the massive works of earth-movers like Michael Heizer and Robert Smithson, for whom an unpeopled landscape provided an empty canvas to be reshaped into a giant sculpture. Instead, Miss’s works were designed to alter viewers’ perspectives as they moved through and around the site, discovering unexpected vistas and pathways.

One work from this period, Perimeters/Pavilions/Decoys (1977–78), temporarily installed across four acres at the Nassau County Museum in Roslyn, New York, embodied the kind of paradoxes Miss was pursuing. Three towers and two semicircular mounds drew viewers forward, but the bulk of the work was not visible on the surface of the land. Instead, a ladder between the mounds invited viewers underground, where they encountered a series of corridors, doors, and slotlike windows.

The work caught the eye of the formidable critic Rosalind Krauss, whose now-classic 1979 essay “Sculpture in the Expanded Field” helped launch Miss into the limelight. In it, Krauss laid out a framework for understanding the movement of sculpture beyond the realm of the object and into an entanglement with architecture and landscape. She opened the essay with a description of Perimeters/Pavilions/Decoys, using it to illustrate the deconstruction of the idea of sculpture and its replacement, the idea of the field. In her typically abstruse fashion, Krauss noted, “Thus the field provides both for an expanded but finite set of related positions for a given artist to occupy and explore, and for an organization of work that is not dictated by the conditions of a particular medium.”

Miss was grateful for the attention, but remarks wryly, “I don’t know what she was interested in, and I didn’t know then what she was talking about. And she obviously didn’t really mean the ‘expanded’ field, because as soon as I started doing that, I went off her radar.” In fact, while Krauss’s essay mapped a set of formal relations, Miss was expanding the field of art to address human concerns.

The artist’s attention shifted from rural to urban settings, particularly to New York City, where she lived and worked. Her projects involved deep research into a site’s history, context, and present use, and these issues helped shape her concepts. For instance, when she was invited to work with the architects who were renovating the Union Square subway station in 1992, she delved into the archeology of the station and discovered how greatly original features like stairs, lighting fixtures, mosaic tiles, and turnstiles had fallen into disrepair or been altered or replaced over the years. She created 125 red framing elements that marked these changes, highlighting historical details that passersby might otherwise miss. At Battery Park City’s South Cove, pilings, blue ships’ lanterns hung on wooden lampposts, and native rocks and plants bring New York City’s residents back in touch with the area’s previous life as an important port. In a playful touch, the shape of the overlook platform deliberately mimics the crown of the Statue of Liberty, which is visible across the water.

In 1989, Miss embarked on a pivotal project—one that recently made headlines. She was invited by the Des Moines Art Center to create something in the nearby Greenwood Pond. Once an important community gathering place, the pond had deteriorated and become overrun with green algae. She started by researching the site, speaking with local ecologists, historians, and Meskwaki Nation elders. Miss discovered that the site had once contained a wetland, and that it, like most of Iowa’s wetlands, had been drained and tilled for agriculture. She also investigated the history and lives of the Meskwaki tribe, which had once occupied this land.

From that research, Miss decided to restore the pond while transforming it into a kind of demonstration wetland, using forms that echoed Native American lodges and earthen mounds. This work, titled Greenwood Pond: Double Site (1989–96), comprised a network of paths, canopies, and mounds, including a wooden pavilion following the curve of the shore as well as an elevated lookout and a walkway that plunged below the surface, allowing visitors to sit beneath the water level. As a newly restored wetland, it welcomed birds, insects, wildlife, and native plants in an educational display of biodiversity.

For this project, Miss collaborated with a wide range of community members and groups. She reflects that Greenwood Pond: Double Site “was kind of a fulcrum for me, because I was able to have a scientist, the Science Center, the Founders Garden Club, the Parks Department, the museum, all in conversation. It was a different way of working.”

For Jenny Dixon, director emerita of the Noguchi Museum and the inaugural director of the Public Art Fund, this “different way of working” is what makes Miss “a major figure in this field.” Dixon, who has known Miss since the 1970s, adds that “you just can’t make a piece of public art in your studio. There has to be another piece to it, whether it’s community, architecture, the space, the site, the environment. Mary’s not one-dimensional.”

With Greenwood Pond: Double Site, Miss began to shift from her earlier focus on land and history to the kinds of ecological issues that subsequently have become her primary focus. “In the wake of climate change,” she remarks, “artists need to help redefine nature. I’ve long claimed that the city is nature and we are living in it.”

But the project was also pivotal for a less happy reason: While the work was enjoyed by the public and maintained by the Art Center through various administrations, by 2024 it had fallen into disrepair. Kelly Baum, the Art Center’s director, informed Miss that the work would need to be dismantled because the Art Center did not have the $8 million necessary for restoration and future maintenance. A public uproar and lawsuit ensued as Miss attempted to force the museum to raise the funds to save the work. In the end, a settlement was reached: The work was taken down, and Miss was awarded $900,000, a portion of which she earmarked for a newly established fund to support threatened site-specific art works.

AT LEAST GREENWOODPOND: DOUBLE SITE was realized and lasted for 29 years. For Miss, the 1990s were marked by increasing frustration as many projects that had consumed considerable time and effort never came to fruition. These included projects for New York City; Los Angeles; St. Paul; St. Louis; and Long Beach, California. Many other projects that did see the light of day were then altered or diminished by the architects and planners with whom she was working.

Then came September 11, 2001. Miss’s studio was in the shadow of the Twin Towers. She recalls that her place “was covered with dust because the windows had been open. We had to come in and clean everything.” Deeply aware of the need for collective mourning, she soon came up with the idea of creating a temporary project to establish places where people could gather and lay flowers. Working with architects Elliott Maltby and Victoria Marshall, Miss named the work Moving Perimeter: A Wreath for Ground Zero (2002). She was struck by the motley nature of the temporary barriers and fences—composed of wood, chain link, concrete, and pipe—that kept out people making a pilgrimage to the site. Her idea was to paint some of these blue and replace others with new sections of fencing, seating, planting, and blue lights in order to create a movable perimeter that would grow denser and smaller as the exclusion zone decreased. It would operate as a place for people to gather and connect and share the profound sense of loss that was enveloping the city.

After years of struggling with bureaucracies, distant deadlines, and ultimately rejections, this project was an eye-opener for Miss. Although it was never realized, it still allowed her to rethink what public art could be: If it was temporary, it could be executed quickly and flexibly. And it would help if it relied on willing participants rather than reluctant bureaucrats to give the work meaning. “Wait a second,” Miss says she realized at one point. “There’s a way I can function instead of just waiting to get something built. You just need to take charge.”

While she was still working on some large-scale permanent projects during this period, she began to think in terms of interactive, collaborative, open-ended works that left a light footprint. This culminated in 2010 with the inauguration of CaLL, based on a concept that went all the way back to her idea of art as a net stretched across communities or even whole cities. She describes CaLL as “the idea that artists, working in collaboration with scientists, engineers, planners, and communities, can have an essential role to play in addressing the pressing environmental issues of our times.”

Over the past 15 years, CaLL has evolved into a series of different practices, including workshops, urban walks, programs, and community-initiated projects. The most ambitious of these is an initiative with the city of Milwaukee called WaterMarks (2015–). Working with ecologist Eric Sanderson, she has focused on the city’s complex relationship with water. A source of life, water also poses dangers from flooding, contamination, and aging infrastructures. WaterMarks involves going into neighborhoods to explore their specific relationship to water, engaging local residents, artists, and public and private institutions along the way. The project coalesces around a set of markers, 25-foot poles wrapped with solar panels and topped by illuminated blue letters chosen by the community.

These poles map out the nexus for educational and artistic projects that center on local stewardship of water. For instance, at Milwaukee’s Acosta Middle School, students chose the letter A for their marker, standing both for Acosta and, in this Latino area, agua, or water. In a series of walks with experts through the neighborhood, students learned about the area’s water history, its green infrastructure, and the need for storm water management in an urban environment. A green space created on a remediated slice of abandoned industrial wasteland became the Green Tech Station, designated with the letter G. This is a site for development, demonstration, and education about green infrastructure projects relating to the management of invasive species, pollutants, and flooding. The letter H designates the Harbor View neighborhood, where enjoyment of water includes activities like swimming, fishing, and kayaking.

So far, WaterMarks has 14 markers in place. Together they form what Miss describes as an atlas of water for the city. Rising above the network of markers is a 350-foot stack in the center of the city’s water treatment plant that has been repurposed as a beacon, with changing colors that warn residents when they need to restrict their water usage to avoid possible sewage overflows into Lake Michigan. “One really wonderful thing that’s come out of this 10-year project,” Miss says, “is that there’s a collective of people who are going to take it forward.”

WaterMarks is the most fully realized of CaLL’s undertakings, but the ideas it exemplifies appear in projects in other cities as well. In Boulder, Colorado, in 2007, Miss worked with climate scientists to map the potential disaster of the long-anticipated “500-year flood” with metal discs on trees, fences, and buildings that charted predicted water levels. In Indianapolis in 2014, Miss worked with local organizations to install along the White River more than 100 markers and mirrors that employed technologies like apps, webcam projections, and websites to provide information on the river’s history and ecology.

Building on Miss’s earlier projects in Manhattan, CaLL has also undertaken several initiatives in New York City. Broadway: 1000 Steps, initiated in 2009, is an ongoing reenvisioning of the city’s longest street as a “green corridor” equipped with hubs that provide text, images, and technology designed to engage the public in dialogues about environmental issues. In 2016, CaLL started Rescuing Tibbetts Brook in the Bronx. This project focuses on a plan to unearth a long-buried stream that now feeds into the sewer system and diverts it to a corridor adjacent to the Major Deegan Expressway, where it can become a new linear park.

Miss is currently working in Corona, Queens, on Cloudburst. This project charts the path taken by water during recent devastating flooding events in this low-lying immigrant neighborhood and helps residents understand the dangers as well as the mitigation efforts being undertaken by the city’s Department of Environmental Protection (DEP). CaLL has organized an advisory committee comprised of local residents and institutions, which will work with the DEP to pinpoint problems and needs. Central to Cloudburst are walks, either led by experts or self-guided, that explore the area’s ecology, specifically its water problems and possible solutions. In a move reminiscent of the markers set up in Milwaukee, beneath trees along the path, CaLL has placed benches with signs that invite passersby to connect to informative recordings via the internet.

But it’s the templates these projects create that are the most meaningful—at least for Olivia Georgia, who has been executive director of CaLL since early in its inception, in 2010. “It’s a model that can be refreshed, enhanced, and amplified by other artists,” she says. “We can’t imagine what the other artists’ projects will be. We set the table for them, but they have to figure out how to use the instruments in the tool kit we have assembled.”

Miss came up as part of a generation benefiting from federal support for the arts; but since that time, the tides have very much turned. She recently had grants canceled by both the National Science Foundation and the National Endowment for the Arts. “They’re saying, ‘No science. No climate change,’” she explains, adding that this backlash makes it all the more important to find self-sustaining ways to help people connect to environmental issues. “I’ve had to really change my practice. I loved doing the Des Moines project. I love doing these projects that cost a lot of money. But now I’m trying to find a different way…. I’ve gone from this young woman who wanted to be acknowledged as an artist and who wanted to be able to make a mark with the work that I did, and I’ve ended up wanting this CaLL work to continue. That’s what I would really love to be able to leave behind.

“I really got beaten down in the ’90s,” she reflects, “and yet I ended up in this place. I did come out from under it. I’m glad I reached my optimism again.”

La-Z-Boy (LZB) Q2 2027 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Joybird's 30% sales drop is weighing on an otherwise growing segment mix.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 18, 2026 at 1:08PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

La-Z-Boy (LZB +3.15%) reported second quarter results featuring sales growth in the Retail and Wholesale segments. Management attributed top-line performance to store acquisitions and new store openings while operating margins reflected supply chain investments and onetime warranty adjustments. The company is executing a multiyear distribution and home delivery transformation and manufacturing plant consolidations aimed at improving long-term cost structures.

Operator: Good day, everyone, and welcome to the La-Z-Boy Fiscal 2027 Second Quarter Conference Call. [Operator Instructions]. It is now my pleasure to hand the floor over to your host, Mark Becks, Director of Investor Relations and Corporate Development. Sir, the floor is yours.

Mark Becks: Thank you, Matthew. Good morning, everyone, and thanks for joining us to discuss our fiscal 2027 2nd quarter. Joining me on today's call are Melinda Whittington, La-Z-Boy Incorporated Board Chair, President and Chief Executive Officer; and Taylor Luebke, SVP and CFO. Melinda will open and close the call, and Taylor will speak to segment performance and the financials midway through. After our prepared remarks, we will open the line for questions. Slides will accompany this presentation, and you may view them through our webcast link, which will be available for 1 year. A telephone replay of the call will be available for 1 week beginning this afternoon.

I would like to remind you that some statements made in today's call include forward-looking statements about La-Z-Boy's future performance and other matters. Although we believe these statements to be reasonable, our actual results could vary materially. The most significant risk factors that could affect our future results are described in our annual report on Form 10-K. We encourage you to review those risk factors as well as other key information detailed in our SEC filings. Also, our earnings release is available under the News and Events tab on the Investor Relations page of our website, and it includes reconciliations of certain adjusted measures, which are also included as an appendix at the end of our conference call slide deck.

With that, I will now turn the call over to Melinda.

Melinda Whittington: Thanks, Mark. Good morning, everyone. Yesterday, following the close of market, we reported solid October-ended second quarter results. We delivered continued strong sales growth in Retail and returned to growth in Wholesale, excluding the impact of the wholesale casegoods divestiture, even as the broader environment remained choppy. We continue to benefit from expansion of our retail business through new and acquired stores and strong in-store execution and from growth in our wholesale business through strategic partnerships. Additionally, we continue to advance our major initiatives and remain on track to realize these benefits over time. This quarter provides further proof in our strategy to grow retail and invest to position our company for long-term value creation.

Highlights for our second quarter included Retail written sales increasing 11%. Retail delivered sales increasing 14%, led by acquisitions and new stores with delivered same-store sales roughly flat. We added 3 company-owned stores during the quarter, including 1 new store and 2 acquired, bringing our total to 237 company-owned stores or 62% of the total store network. We also signed an agreement to acquire one additional independent La-Z-Boy store. Wholesale delivered sales increased 3%, excluding the impact of the casegoods divestiture, led by growth in our core North America La-Z-Boy wholesale business, which has now grown 5 of the last 7 quarters.

Bringing it all together, total company delivered consolidated sales increased 4%, excluding the impact of the wholesale casegoods divestiture. Adjusted operating margin was 5%. Cash flow continued to be strong. We returned $32 million to shareholders through share repurchases and dividends, triple the prior year second quarter amount, and we announced a 12% dividend increase, marking our sixth consecutive year of double-digit dividend increases. I'm pleased with the quarter's strong top line performance and solid execution while balancing the impact of multiple strategic investments in process. Our focus on bringing innovative, high-quality handcrafted products to market and delivering an inspiring end-to-end consumer experience is strengthening connections with existing consumers and introducing La-Z-Boy to new audiences.

I am confident in our ability to continue to grow our business even in this dynamic environment. Turning to written trends. Second quarter total written sales for our company-owned retail segment increased 11% versus last year's second quarter, driven by acquired and new stores. Written same-store sales, which exclude the benefit of new and acquired stores, declined slightly for the quarter. We were pleased with positive same-store sales trends for the combined August and September period, highlighting the strength of our Labor Day execution. However, this momentum was offset by the particularly soft October trends seen across many consumer businesses.

While consumer trends continue to be quite choppy, we have seen positive momentum in early November as we enter the key holiday sales season. Our Joybird business continues to experience the most significant volatility with written sales decreasing 30% in the quarter as we rightsize marketing spend levels. We continue to take decisive action to improve the resiliency and cost structure of this business, including transitioning Joybird production into our established U.S. plant network by the end of our third quarter. While early, we also continue to test additional go-to-market adjustments to enable sustainable profit accretive growth, including dedicated Joybird branded space in a small number of La-Z-Boy stores as well as continued adjustments to our marketing approach.

As we prepare to celebrate La-Z-Boy's 100th anniversary in March, we are strengthening our century-long heritage of comfort, quality, customization and quick speed to market. We remain agile and focused on leveraging our competitive advantages to drive growth for the next 100 years. Even as the macroeconomic backdrop remains challenging, we believe the strength of our iconic brand, retail operations and vertically integrated supply chain enables us to navigate the near-term, driving sales growth and margin accretion while investing for the long-term sustainable growth. I want to take a moment to highlight progress on our strategic investments.

In Retail, during the second quarter, we added 3 company-owned stores, including 1 new store in La Crosse, Wisconsin, and 2 acquired stores in New Orleans, bringing our total to 237 company-owned stores, which is nearly double our company-owned store count just 10 years ago and bringing corporate ownership up to 62% of the total La-Z-Boy store network of 380 stores. This Retail expansion gives us greater control of the end-to-end consumer experience where we can inspire and delight more shoppers. We continue to see an opportunity to expand to roughly 450 locations across North America at a rate of approximately 10 new stores annually, most of which will be company-owned.

Independent dealer acquisitions remain another important growth lever as these transactions are immediately sales and profit accretive and often offer additional growth opportunities in underpenetrated markets. During the quarter, we signed an agreement to acquire another one additional independent La-Z-Boy store in Madison, Wisconsin, that is expected to close in December. Over the last year, we have acquired 3 independent dealers and 20 independent stores, and we see continued opportunity across our nearly 40 independent dealers and over 140 independent stores still in our network. Our digital transformation continues to advance as an important enabler to expand La-Z-Boy brand reach, enhancing how consumers discover, explore and engage with the brand online.

This is creating a stronger connection between online inspiration and in-store experience. We recently launched redesigned product detail pages across key upholstery categories, making it easier for consumers to explore our products, personalize their selections and move seamlessly through the purchase journey. The new experience includes enhanced 3D product imagery, higher definition fabric and cover visualizations, simpler navigation and customization and more prominent add-to-cart functionality. Importantly, since launching the new product pages, add-to-cart rates have continued to improve, providing another proof point that these investments are strengthening consumer engagement and helping create a more connected digital experience.

We're encouraged by progress in our digital transformation with meaningful improvements in traffic, conversion and sales online and our more connected experience to also drive growth into our retail stores. We're also thoughtfully advancing our use of AI across the enterprise with a focus on enhancing the consumer experience and driving enhanced analysis and efficiencies. We are advancing work to ensure product and brand discoverability as consumers' search behavior evolves and building capabilities around our data and internal processes to make the omnichannel journey more intuitive, seamless and efficient. While technology is enhancing how we serve our consumers, our people remain at the heart of the La-Z-Boy experience.

We are proud that La-Z-Boy was once again named to Forbes 2027 Best Customer Service list for the fifth consecutive year. This award recognizes our team's passion for delivering the transformational power of comfort and creating an experience commensurate with our iconic brand from online and in-store to in-home and after care. To expand a little more on our journey with the consumer, we continue to strengthen the La-Z-Boy brand through consumer-led marketing and innovation, using deeper insights to better understand how consumers live, shop and engage with our brand. Those insights are helping shape our product assortment across styles, features, price points and life stages.

We continue to see a more bifurcated consumer in the marketplace, and we have optimized our product assortment, pricing and messaging to attract both higher-end design consumers as well as those consumers looking for a value-focused entrance into our brand lineup. Recent introductions include our expanded power lift assortment with enhanced comfort and functionality; AudioLuxe, our premium audio furniture line featuring sound by Klipsch, which is now available in select stores; and Comfort Essentials, an opening price point offering within our stationary assortment.

Together, these innovations create clear opening price point and trade-up options, extend La-Z-Boy into more rooms and homes and allow us to reach a broader audience while remaining rooted in the comfort, quality and customization that has defined our brand for generations. In the Wholesale segment, we continue to drive growth in our core North American La-Z-Boy Wholesale business, targeting a broader consumer reach. We remain focused on growing with compatible strategic partners that value the comfort and quality we deliver to consumers and the traffic that La-Z-Boy can drive to their stores. We ended the quarter with over 1,400 La-Z-Boy Comfort Studio and branded space locations, each with dedicated space for La-Z-Boy branded products.

Our final strategic pillar, strengthening our foundational capabilities continues to advance with our multiyear distribution and home delivery transformation and our manufacturing plant consolidation. Our vertically integrated model with over 95% of upholstered furniture produced domestically is a key competitive advantage, allowing us to deliver customized products within 4 to 6 weeks while limiting our relative exposure to trade policy volatility. During the second quarter, we completed the planned exit of one of our facilities, and we have accelerated our plans to consolidate our Joybird manufacturing plant into our U.S. network by the end of our third quarter.

Separately, our distribution and home delivery transformation, now in year 2 of a 4-year project, will optimize our footprint from 15 distribution centers to 3 centralized hubs, enabling 20% less mileage traveled and 30% less warehouse square footage and doubling our delivery radius to consumers. The remaining 2 of our centralized hubs will open in Missouri and Tennessee by the end of our fiscal year, and we will complete our transition into these new sites throughout our fiscal '28.

These significant supply chain investments will create an even stronger company by expanding the value we offer to consumers through greater reach and speed of delivery, supporting our communities through jobs and localized investment and driving value for shareholders by improving our cost structure and working capital productivity. We remain focused on creating our own momentum, controlling what we can control and driving value for all our stakeholders. While the timing of an industry recovery remains uncertain, we are well positioned to continue to grow regardless of the external environment and further accelerate that growth when housing trends improve. And now let me turn the call over to Taylor to review the financial results in more detail. Taylor?

Taylor Luebke: Thank you, Melinda, and good morning, everyone. As a reminder, we present our results on both a GAAP and adjusted basis. We believe the adjusted presentation better reflects underlying operating trends and performance of the business. Adjusted results exclude items which are detailed in our press release and in the appendix section of our conference call slides. On a consolidated basis, fiscal 2027 second quarter sales were $526 million, up 1% as reported and up 4% adjusting for the impact of the wholesale casegoods divestiture, which was completed in May. Consolidated GAAP operating income was $22 million and adjusted operating income was $26 million.

Consolidated GAAP operating margin was 4.1% and adjusted operating margin was 5% versus 7.1% last year, with the change driven by strategic investments in our supply chain, including plant consolidations and distribution transformation, both in progress and a 110 basis point impact from a onetime change in our dealer warranty arrangements, which benefited last year's second quarter. Diluted earnings per share totaled $0.45 on a GAAP basis and adjusted diluted EPS was $0.53. As I move to the segment discussion, my comments from here will focus on our adjusted reporting unless specifically stated otherwise. Starting with the Retail segment.

For the second quarter, delivered sales increased 14% to $252 million, driven by growth from acquired and new stores with delivered same-store sales roughly flat versus a year ago. Retail adjusted operating margin was 9.7% versus 10.7% last year, driven by a shift in product mix, along with investment in targeted promotional activity. For our Wholesale segment, delivered sales decreased 2% to $362 million versus last year on a reported basis and increased 3% adjusting for the impact of the casegoods divestiture. Sales were led by strong growth in our core North America La-Z-Boy Wholesale business.

Adjusted operating margin for the Wholesale segment was 5.9% in the second quarter versus 8.1% last year due to strategic investments in our supply chain, including plant consolidations and distribution transformation, both in progress and a 150 basis point impact from a onetime change in our dealer warranty arrangements, which benefited last year's second quarter, partially offset by favorable net tariff impact, including refunds and pricing actions net of tariff costs. For Joybird, reported in Corporate and Other, delivered sales were $25 million, down 29% on lower delivered sales volume and reduced marketing investment against a challenged consumer backdrop.

The Corporate and Other adjusted operating loss increased versus the prior year, primarily due to expense deleverage on lower Joybird delivered sales as we continue to work to exit the stand-alone plant and minimize fixed costs. Moving on to our consolidated adjusted gross margin and SG&A performance for fiscal 2027 second quarter. Consolidated adjusted gross margin for the entire company increased 50 basis points versus the prior year second quarter. The increase in gross margin was primarily driven by the shift in consolidated mix towards our Retail segment, which has a higher gross margin rate than our Wholesale segment and favorable net tariff impact, including refunds and pricing actions net of tariff costs.

This was partially offset by strategic investments in our supply chain, including the plant consolidations and distribution transformation, both in progress. Adjusted SG&A as a percent of sales for the quarter increased by 260 basis points compared with last year, also due to the shift in consolidated mix towards our Retail segment, which carries a higher fixed cost structure relative to Wholesale and 110 basis points impact from a onetime change in our dealer warranty arrangements, which benefited last year's second quarter. Our effective income tax rate on a GAAP basis for the quarter was 26% versus 26.7% for the prior year period.

We continue to expect a normalized effective income tax rate for the full year in the range of 26% to 27%. Our balance sheet remains strong with $247 million in cash and no externally funded debt. We generated $43 million in cash from operating activities in the quarter. We invested $36 million back into the business with $31 million in capital expenditures related to our distribution transformation, manufacturing-related investments, in new La-Z-Boy stores and remodels as well as $5 million for a 2-store retail acquisition. We also returned $32 million to shareholders, triple the prior year amount, including $22 million in share repurchases and $10 million in dividends.

We have $269 million remaining on this authorization and continue to view share repurchases as an important vehicle to deliver shareholder returns. Also, as of yesterday, the Board of Directors declared a quarterly cash dividend of $0.27 per share, a 12% increase over the previous dividend and our sixth consecutive year of double-digit increases. Our capital allocation strategy remains consistent to reinvest 50% of operating cash flow back into the business and return 50% to shareholders in share repurchases and dividends. Before turning the call back to Melinda, let me highlight several important items for our third quarter and fiscal year.

Planning prudently given uneven consumer demand, we expect third quarter enterprise sales to be in the range of $505 million to $525 million. We expect third quarter adjusted operating margin to remain in the range of 4% to 5.5%, reflecting a measured view of the macroeconomic backdrop and continued near-term strategic investments in process as we optimize our plant footprint and progress our distribution transformation. Separately, we continue to monitor trade and tariff policy and adjust accordingly. As of the end of the second quarter, we are substantially done with the process for refunds. As a reminder, over 95% of our upholstery production is based in the U.S., which continues to be a competitive advantage.

We continue to expect capital expenditures to be in the range of $90 million to $110 million for the year, with this being the largest investment year for our distribution transformation as well as ongoing investments in manufacturing in our La-Z-Boy retail stores, including new stores and remodels. We expect capital allocation to be balanced between investments back into the business and return to shareholders, including a normalized pace of share repurchases. And with that, I will turn the call back to Melinda.

Melinda Whittington: Thanks, Taylor. As we look ahead, I'm energized by the opportunities in front of us. This March, we will celebrate our 100-year anniversary. We are extremely proud of our iconic brand, our strong financial foundation, our clear strategy for the future and our dedicated team that will deliver our next chapter. The investments we are making across the enterprise are designed to make La-Z-Boy Incorporated an even stronger company and build on our rich legacy. While the external environment remains dynamic, we are focused on what we can control and confident in our ability to continue creating our own momentum, gaining share and delivering sustainable, profitable growth and long-term value for our shareholders.

Mark Becks: Thank you, Melinda. We will begin the question-and-answer period now. Matthew, please review the instructions for getting into the queue to ask questions.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Our first question is coming from Bobby Griffin from Raymond James.

Robert Griffin: Melinda, I wanted to maybe just start with just kind of what you're seeing from the broad base of your different customers. And I guess I'm asking in context of Wholesale ex the divestiture showed a little growth this quarter, which was an improvement from what we saw last quarter. It looks like Retail, if you take out October, has seen some life in, call it, the prior 2 months before October as well as the early November period because you called out a little bit of an improvement there. So, just can you maybe expand on kind of what you're seeing from the different channels and the customers?

And then as a second part of that, is what we're -- is some of that improvement more price driven? Or are we actually starting to see some underlying volume stabilization or even improvement?

Melinda Whittington: Yes. Great question, Bobby. Let me take a couple of pieces of that. First of all, again, uneven, choppy is sort of the word of the day. Overall, I feel good about, even in this pretty dynamic environment, where our brand stands both in our own stores and with our wholesale customers. As you called out, we're back to growth in our core North America business on Wholesale as well. That's been 5 of the last 7 quarters we've grown there as well as continuing to see our Retail expansion.

What we do continue to see is those peaks of the holiday periods are where consumers are increasingly buying, particularly thinking about sort of the more challenged consumer, the value-seeking consumer. You need to be sharp on those holidays and then those valleys in between. Enticing the consumer into making a significant purchase continues to be challenging. That unevenness between holidays and the non-peak periods just continues to be more dramatic. It's important to win on those holidays and then, of course, to find ways, particularly with our design consumers and those troughs in between.

Other than that, within Wholesale, you get even a little bit more of that -- a little bit more choppiness because you're balanced by also how your wholesale customers are balancing their inventory. I think that just exacerbates that situation a bit. To your point, we saw a solid Labor Day, and we've seen that across our consumer -- our customer base and those folks that aren't our customers, but we speak with on an informal basis at market and our industry events. We're seeing those very consistent kind of trends. We're focused on driving a strong holiday season now, as we said, improved from October trends going into November. October, it's a little bit hard to call on.

Was there election malaise? Is it a broader trend around the consumer? And so, time will tell on that side of things because to your point, in September, we were feeling fairly good coming out of out of Labor Day. So, that's why we're guiding prudently going into the next couple of quarters, recognizing that there more remains to be seen on sort of the choppiness of the consumer, but we feel good about the opportunities within our own stores and with our wholesale customers over time.

Robert Griffin: Okay. That's helpful. And then my follow-up is for Taylor. Taylor, you probably could guess I'm going to ask a margin question here. But obviously, a lot of moving parts this year. You're lapping the warranty last year in 2Q. I think you lap kind of a similar sized benefit with some inventory aspects in 4Q. And then at the same time, the last 2 quarters have had tariff refunds in that we can kind of size a little bit net of pricing from the 10-Qs. How do we think about the base of just kind of where the underlying operations is going into next year?

Should we be backing out these tariff refunds and kind of looking at that as the underlying base? Or was there less pricing pass-through because the tariff refunds helped offset some of the higher cost environment? Just any color on kind of clean up some of the noise where we can think about the underlying level of margins here. Understandably, you're also doing a supply chain transformation at the same time, so there's cost there, too.

Taylor Luebke: Yes. I was expecting, and thanks for the question, Bobby. Let me try to answer it in a couple of ways. First, I'd start with we have a lot of big investments in the business continuing to be in progress. Not all of those just stop on a dime as we enter our next fiscal year. Some will transition into the next, but we continue to feel proud and pleased with our strategy of one, growing our sales in light of kind of the macro pressure with continuing to expand new stores, remodels as well as grow our Wholesale business, but also the significant supply chain initiatives, both the plant consolidation as well as the distribution transformation.

As you alluded to, obviously, over the last several quarters and even year-over-year, there's been a lot of puts and takes, some of our own investment into the business, but also some other, call it, more onetime. I'd harken back to our La-Z-Boy Wholesale business, which is the largest part of that segment is generally running on an annualized basis, about 50 basis points behind on gross margin given all those puts and takes. We think that's where we'll revert back to once we complete some of these sizable transitions and get our U.S.-based manufacturing back up to the level of productivity. I think we'll see that as we enter and progress into next fiscal year.

Then just another general enterprise perspective, even in light of, call it, broader industry challenges over the last 4 years where housing continues to be frozen. The entire company, we've shown a pretty good resilience of being able to maintain a total kind of, call it, 7% to mid-7% margin range as we progress these initiatives, both on supply chain, continue to grow our business and also rightsize and sustainably adjust the Joybird business, full confidence that's where we'll progress back to. It doesn't happen overnight, no, but we feel good about how we're entering next fiscal year.

Operator: Your next question is coming from Brad Thomas from KeyBanc Capital Markets.

Taylor Zick: It's Taylor Zick on for Brad here. Melinda, I just want to clarify a comment you made and it was in the press release as well. You noted the improvement in trends here, I guess, so far into November relative to where they were being a little bit softer in October. So, was that comment purely an improvement from October itself? Or have you seen trends kind of inflect kind of where fiscal 2Q kind of came in here?

Melinda Whittington: Yes. I guess taking a look -- I guess what I'd say is we're 2, 3 weeks in at this point on November. Broadly, we were positive in August and September in totality adjusting for kind of how Labor Day fell. Then we really saw things fall off pretty dramatically in October and back to more normalized here in the early days of November. We had a solid Veterans Day execution, and now we're into the month of Black Friday. So, pleased with overall a stabilization, but we're early in the quarter and hence, some of the prudence in how we're guiding into the quarter.

Taylor Zick: Got you. Okay. Understood. And then maybe just to maybe ask a question here about the Wholesale segment, maybe to kind of build off of what Bobby had asked. Pretty strong growth here accelerating when you kind of exclude the divestitures. Was there any benefit during the second quarter related to any of the timing orders being pushed out of 1Q into 2Q? And then if there were, are you willing to quantify that here for us?

Taylor Luebke: No, there was no phasing or timing of orders impacting the quarter. We ended our Q1 with what we would say is a solid backlog, but it wasn't like a big deviation from what we've seen in the past. It was just good execution, more stabilized flow-through of our growth with our kind of strategic partners across our Wholesale business.

Taylor Zick: Okay. And then maybe if I could just squeeze one more quick one here. Melinda, maybe can you speak a little bit about the promotional environment across the industry? We've seen promotions kind of being longer in duration, but not necessarily deeper in terms of discounting. I guess what are you seeing here around the holidays? What do you expect? And maybe how do you feel about the promotions heading into next calendar year?

Melinda Whittington: Yes. I think very similar to what you just said, Taylor, in that we are seeing -- we joked about the month of Labor Day, and now we'll move into the month of Black Friday. The reality is that there are a lot of consumers out there that are seeking value, and we know that our products are aspirational to be into the comfort and quality and customization that we can deliver. We are seeing that. We definitely saw that in Labor Day, and we expect that to continue into Black Friday with the need to have sharp opening price points, sharp promotions that go narrow and deep.

But at the same time, our upper end consumer that is still involved in design and lots of upgrades, and I always talk about the powers and the leathers and the whole room solutions remains very strong. I would say more of the same as kind of what we're expecting. The reality is, in our industry, input costs haven't lessened per se. It doesn't leave a lot of room for anybody to do anything really, really crazy and dramatic. But at the same time, you got to be sharp to be able to entice that consumer into your stores.

Operator: Our next question is coming from Anthony Lebiedzinski from Sidoti.

Anthony Lebiedzinski: So, as it relates to the October trends, some of the companies in this space have talked about -- and I know you touched on this a little bit, Melinda. But as far as the pre-election environment with retailers unable to really effectively advertise on TV or other means. Is that something that you -- did you guys also kind of pull back on your marketing spending because of the political environment? Just wondering if you could just expand on that.

Melinda Whittington: Yes. I think, Anthony, the October is not a real heavy month for us anyways in furniture, right? If you think about really the big holiday tent poles are really more around Labor Day, and then we're back into it a little bit with Veterans Day and into Black Friday. By definition, we weren't as noisy in October nor would we be in a regular time. Then as you say, I mean, across any industry, whenever you're building up to an election, you know that it's hard to get the attention of the consumer. Yes, did that play into it? Absolutely, but some of that is just our own seasonality for the industry.

Anthony Lebiedzinski: Got you. So, as we think about the outlook for fiscal Q3, how are you thinking about sales for each of the segments? And if you -- just -- is it kind of more of the same as we saw in fiscal Q2? And how do we think about the organic or pro forma sales, excluding the prior divestitures?

Taylor Luebke: I would just hit a couple of things, Anthony, and thanks for the question. One is just generally speaking, our Q3 and Q2 from a shape of the entire business are generally pretty close, just as you look back and kind of get rid of some of the onetimes here or there, but generally the same size, generally the same cost structure overall. I think it's important just as we outlook the next kind of -- next 3 months is to recognize the October softness we had mentioned does translate to flow through and deliver within the quarter, which is also incorporated in kind of the range of outcomes we put out there with our guidance.

As it concerns to each of our segments, we continue to target and our objective is to organically grow our business. With that being said, we will be lapping our sizable retail acquisition that was completed almost the first day of last Q3. So, you'll see some of those retail growth rates year-over-year moderate as that's now in our base.

Anthony Lebiedzinski: Got you. Okay. All right. And then as far as Joybird, so that's been the most challenged business here. So, kind of can you just walk us through some of the actions you're taking to kind of improve sales in that segment?

Melinda Whittington: Certainly. I'll start by saying we still see Joybird as having a unique consumer proposition, and that's why we continue to kind of retool this business and how we go to market. The drag on the bottom line has certainly been a challenge, particularly in a world where that consumer is a little more younger urban consumer has been more affected by some of the overall kind of macroeconomic challenges. The biggest thing we're doing right now is minimizing fixed costs.

As we've talked about, we are taking what was once a stand-alone plant in Mexico for Joybird, and that is the second of our 2 plant consolidations and moving that into our operations within the U.S. at our long-standing plants. Pleased that, that transition is going really well. We've actually moved that up a quarter and expect that to be done here in our Q3 versus our Q4. That just minimizes fixed costs. But more importantly, it's about how do we go to market and how do we ensure that we have a brand that can turn to growth. Right now, on top of the challenged consumer, we have rightsized marketing expenses.

So, that's causing a more dramatic top line impact in the short-term as we kind of retool that approach. But at the same time, we do expect the brand to get back to growth. Some of the other things we're doing is we're experimenting with a store-within-a-store concept with a handful of La-Z-Boy stores that we're bringing a Joybird setting into to kind of experiment with is that a way to delight consumers more broadly, but be really efficient in our floor space. So, more to come. The goal is to get that brand on a growth path and on a profitable path, and we are actively working various pillars against that.

Operator: That concludes our Q&A session. I will now hand the conference back to Mark for closing remarks. Please go ahead.

Mark Becks: Thanks, Matthew. Melinda, Taylor and I will be in our offices to answer any follow-up questions. Thanks, and have a great day.

Operator: Thank you. Everyone, this concludes today's event. You may disconnect at this time, and have a wonderful day. Thank you for your participation.

Macklemore Drops ‘Where Is Our Love’ Song and Video, a Plea for Peace in the Middle East and Other Troubled Areas, Including the U.S.

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Just weeks after controversy erupted around his “Free Palestine” comments onstage while opening for Ed Sheeran, Macklemore has released a new song and video called “Where Is Our Love” that amounts to a plea for peace in the Middle East and other troubled areas across the globe — including the U.S.

The video is filled with imagery from Gaza and other Palestinian areas, some of it heartwarming, some of it disturbing, but there’s no question that the upshot of the message is a plea to stop the killing and oppression — From “Sudan to the Congo, Palestine to Haiti / America being terrorized by ICE agents, quote the Bible and then regurgitate hatred / And what the hell happened to loving thy neighbor / The kids in the Congo mining for cobalt to put into our phones / So we can argue about what genocide is on the internet / As our Teslas drive us home.” Related Stories ‘SNL’ Weekend Update Brutally Roasts Elon Musk Over 'Epic’ Midterms Defeat CNN Sees Some Staff Buyouts Under Skydance Merger Plan

The song itself is in a familiar musical format, with a piano chords and a choir-like chorus intoning the songs title acting as a backdrop for Macklemore’s lyrics — which, it should be noted, contain nothing resembling hate speech. However, any attempt to address such a divisive issue in 2026 is doomed to be misunderstood.

The upshot of the song arrives in its closing lyrics: “And if the words ‘Free Palestine’ are more hurtful than the babies killed / Then I don’t know / But any revolution in the history of the world wasn’t built on hope alone.”

The announcement states that all proceeds from the song will go to the Mother of Hind Fund (named after Hind Rajab, a 5-year-old Palestinian child killed by Israeli forces during the invasion), to support mothers and children in Gaza.

Hyperliquid Pushes for 7 Key Tweaks Ahead of UK’s 2027 Crypto Overhaul

📅 2026-11-18  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

The Hyperliquid Policy Center (HPC) sent a letter to the UK Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) on Nov. 18 with seven recommendations on the draft documents CP26/36 and GC26/7. Three of them concern CP26/36, and four concern GC26/7. The letter was signed by HPC chief executive Jake Chervinsky.

The UK's new crypto regime will start to apply on Oct. 25, 2027. The FCA opened its window for crypto license applications on Sept. 30, 2026, and the window will close on Feb. 28, 2027.

According to the letter, "Regulatory obligations belong with persons who have unilateral control over a function that is a regulated activity." HPC argued that attaching obligations to a person who could not discharge them "would offer consumers no additional protection."

In the letter, HPC also compared public blockchains with the internet. "Like a public blockchain, the internet depends on protocols that identifiable persons maintain and upgrade over time," the group wrote.

HPC asked the FCA to assess each participant in a blockchain network separately. Proprietary traders and market makers "supply much of the liquidity on which order books depend," the group wrote. HPC also asked the regulator to describe compliance requirements as outcomes and to hold regulated firms accountable for them.

On Aug. 24, HPC urged the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) to harmonize their rules for perpetual contracts. The group argued that such products should be classified by their economic structure. According to HPC, a harmonized framework would let exchanges compete on "execution quality and liquidity."

On Sept. 30, HPC sent its response to the European Union's review of the MiCA rules. In it, the group asked the EU to treat crypto perpetual futures as derivatives under MiFID II. "Clarity in application, rather than new legislation, is what would assist in regulating perpetual futures," HPC wrote at the time.

Chervinsky previously served as chief policy officer at the Blockchain Association and as chief legal officer at the crypto venture firm Variant.

What will OpenAI’s new outside safety evaluator tell the public?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Under a four-month pilot, Apollo Research can flag access gaps and explain how redactions affect what the public can verify.

OpenAI is bringing in Apollo Research, an outside AI safety group, for a four-month investigation into whether OpenAI’s models try to bypass safeguards. Apollo can disclose any limits OpenAI places on its access to evidence. OpenAI can redact confidential supporting evidence from the public reports, and Apollo can explain how any redactions affect its conclusions. The agreement protects Apollo’s verdict on each claim from redaction.

Six Apollo evaluators will work inside OpenAI. They will examine whether training encourages models to undermine safeguards, whether safety tests can draw out that behavior, whether it happens during internal use, and whether evaluators can detect the reasoning behind it.

OpenAI confirmed the engagement on November 16 as part of a first phase involving four outside assessors. OpenAI says Apollo is paid for its work and that compensation does not depend on the assessment’s results.

For the training inquiry, Apollo says it will have access to relevant code and datasets and be able to query intermediate training checkpoints. It will also get relevant reinforcement-learning records, including rewards and available reasoning traces. Access details for the other three inquiries were still to be agreed when Apollo announced the pilot on November 16.

The review covers agreed claims, models and time periods. OpenAI can withhold even in-scope material when it considers that reasonably necessary because of technical limits, security risks or intellectual property protection. It must tell Apollo that material was withheld and give written reasons. Where OpenAI considers it feasible and relevant, it will also identify the categories and approximate volume of withheld records. Unfavorable information alone cannot justify withholding access.

Apollo plans two public reports. The first will address whether training encouraged models to undermine safeguards. The second will cover safety testing, internal use and whether the reasoning behind such behavior can be detected. OpenAI can review the reports for confidential material, and its commitments bar it from requesting a redaction solely because a finding is unfavorable. The Safety and Security Committee of the OpenAI Foundation’s board will receive unredacted copies.

The agreement also covers what Apollo learns from employees. OpenAI selects who can discuss confidential information, and formal interviews are logged. The conversations are off the record. To publish a claim arising from an interview, Apollo must establish it independently or obtain a formal attestation from OpenAI.

If OpenAI declines to attest, Apollo can say so publicly and explain the effect on its conclusions while keeping the underlying information confidential.

Apollo can publicly describe the access it requested and received, any limits or delays OpenAI imposed, and the reasons the company gave. It can also discuss the terms of the pilot without first submitting those comments to OpenAI for review.

Grand Theft Auto 6 is "the smoothest GTA game in history" despite 30fps, analysis finds

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GTA 6's frame rate isn't flawless, but it's substantially better than how its predecessors launched

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

GTA 6 is finally here – well, depending on your time zone, anyway. In any case, it's a real video game that people are actually playing, which means it's time to discuss the game's most important aspect: its resolution and frame rate. According to a new tech analysis, Rockstar has managed to coax solid performance out of the current-gen consoles even while pursuing nigh-unprecedented levels of fidelity.

"GTA 6 is a 30 frames per second game, but that also kind of makes it the smoothest GTA game in history, at least at launch," as tech analyst John Linneman explains in a new Digital Foundry video. "Every previous entry ran significantly worse on the original target hardware. Whether that trade-off is worthwhile, however, is entirely up to the individual. But as things stand, I'm not convinced that they could have achieved 60 frames per second in a game of this scope on this class of hardware."

It's now trivial to play previous GTA games at 60fps or above, but each previous 3D entry targeted 30fps on its original platforms and regularly dipped below that target. GTA 6's hold on its 30fps target is much stronger.

On base PS5 and Xbox Series X (which Digital Foundry covered in a separate analysis), the game will dip below 30fps at times when, say, screaming down the road at speed, but those dips are "very minor." If you really try to break the game by heading into a busy intersection with a grenade launcher and causing chaos, you can cause the frame rate to take a more sustained dive, but you'll get a solid experience in the course of normal play.

While you can get some dips with the kind of chaos on PS5 Pro, the whole experience seems to be even smoother on Sony's high-end hardware. "I spent most of my time on the PS5 Pro, which is the best way to play this game by far," Linneman says. "You basically get a near-flawless 30fps update. The frame pacing is perfect, cutscenes consistent and fluid, unlike older Rockstar titles, and everything moves without hiccups or stutters."

Yes, that does also mean Rockstar's finally fixed the cutscene judder that's plagued its games since GTA 4. The miracles continue.

Well, there is one exception, as you probably shouldn't expect any miracles on Xbox Series S. Microsoft's lower-end machine retains most of the graphical features of its more fully-featured counterparts, which I suppose is miraculous in its own way, but its frame rate brings us back to the peak of Xbox 360 gaming, with sustained drops into the 20fps range whenever there's real action on-screen.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

While Rockstar's decision to go 30fps with GTA 6 has been controversial, Linneman is "more willing to forgive it due to just how far it's pushing the visuals." He adds, "it feels like tasting the future."

Check out our full GTA 6 review if you're interested in, you know, the actual game rather than the frame rates.

4. Best pro Xbox: Nacon Revolution X Unlimited

Home Depot’s incoming CEO says homeowners are ‘feeling pressure.’ A quarter can’t cover a $5,000 repair

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Richard McPhail, a 21-year Home Depot veteran who was named the retailer’s next CEO on Tuesday, has been watching financial pressure build among American homeowners. On the company’s third-quarter earnings call the same day, he acknowledged that “a rising interest rate environment puts pressure on our customer and our business.”

McPhail, Home Depot’s CFO since 2019, takes over as CEO on Nov. 23. Ted Decker, who recently returned from a medical leave, will step down as CEO and remain chair of the board.

That’s a problem for a retailer that booked $164.7 billion in revenue last fiscal year, the bulk of it from people fixing up homes they already own. Homeowners are “one of the most resilient customer cohorts in the economy,” McPhail told Yahoo Finance in a February interview. “But they’re feeling pressure like everyone else.”

“If you look at housing turnover, that’s actually been frozen for three years,” he said, adding that turnover has sat at historical lows three years running. He also pointed to customers’ “concerns around housing affordability, general affordability” and “broader concerns over uncertainty and job loss.”

Owning a home has long been considered a path to financial security in America. But even homeowners with considerable housing wealth feel squeezed by the rising cost of living, and some struggle to afford the upkeep of their biggest asset.

Nearly two-thirds of homeowners (63%) say owning a home causes them at least moderate financial stress, up from 55% in June 2025, according to an October survey of 1,000 homeowners conducted for Hometap, a home equity financing company.

That stress comes even as Americans sit on a record $13.2 trillion in accessible home equity as of the second quarter of 2026, according to First American Data & Analytics. That’s the amount owners could borrow against while keeping their combined loan-to-value at 80% or below.

But housing wealth isn’t disposable income. Even owners who locked in rock-bottom pandemic-era mortgage rates aren’t immune to rising property taxes and insurance premiums or climbing utility bills. The typical homeowner now spends $16,566 a year on costs beyond the mortgage, about two-thirds of it on upkeep, according to Zillow.

Job worries are adding to the strain. In the Federal Reserve Bank of New York’s October Survey of Consumer Expectations, released last week, consumers put the odds of higher unemployment a year from now at 45.3%, the highest reading since April 2020. Their perceived chance of losing their own job also ticked up to 13.9%.

A LendingTree survey conducted in September found that 34% of homeowners had postponed at least one necessary repair or maintenance task over the past two years. Among those with outstanding repairs, 32% said they simply couldn’t afford the work.

A surprise $5,000 repair would be entirely out of reach for a quarter of homeowners surveyed. Another 30% could cover it only by substantially draining their savings.

Among homeowners who needed repairs over the past two years, 37% dipped into emergency savings, and 33% cut spending on essentials such as groceries, healthcare, or childcare to pay for the work.

Waiting tends to cost more. Among respondents whose homes currently need repairs, 34% said delaying the work drove up the price, and a quarter said it caused damage elsewhere in the house.

Decker sounded a similar alarm in February, telling analysts that “our customers are telling us that they’re not investing” in bigger projects, citing affordability, the jobs picture, and weak consumer confidence.

Even so, the business has grown this year. Home Depot reported third-quarter sales of $43.4 billion on Tuesday, up 4.9% from a year earlier, with comparable sales up 2.2%. “Third quarter results exceeded our expectations,” McPhail said.

Homeowners may be shelving kitchen remodels, but smaller repairs and maintenance jobs have kept customers coming in. In August, McPhail, then CFO, credited “broad-based demand across the business as customers continued to engage in smaller projects.”

Tight budgets are also pushing homeowners to pick up the tools themselves. Among LendingTree respondents who completed at least some repairs, 60% did some of the work themselves because they couldn’t afford a professional. Among millennials, that figure reached 72%.

At the other end of the market, Home Depot has been courting professional contractors, builders, and remodelers. SRS, the building-materials distributor it acquired for $18.25 billion in June 2024, delivered “almost $5 billion in sales in the quarter,” McPhail said. Executives said on the call that cross-selling between Home Depot and SRS is on track to generate $400 million in sales this year, a figure the company aims to double in 2027.

The company has also been investing in AI and digital tools that help pros plan projects, manage material lists, and track orders. Its express delivery service now reaches customers within two hours from virtually all of its stores, incoming president and COO Ann-Marie Campbell said on the call.

Home Depot now expects total sales to grow about 4.5% in fiscal 2026, with comparable sales up roughly 1.4%. McPhail is betting that speed will keep winning over a cautious customer: “Our investments in express delivery and in AI customer and associate enablement are making a meaningful difference.”

Google’s AI feature in Search gets its own incognito mode

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google’s search engine has long offered an incognito mode for all those search queries you don’t want logged or associated with your account. Now, Google’s AI Mode in Search is getting a similar feature. In a blog post, the company announced a new feature that brings “temporary threads” to AI Mode, where the conversations you have won’t be resurfaced later in areas like your search history or recommendations.

Many leading AI chatbots already offer the option for temporary chats, Google Gemini included. However, there was not yet an option to have a temporary chat when you’re searching Google and are dropped into its conversational interface, known as AI Mode.

Google says that users won’t have to adjust their settings to take advantage of the new option. Instead, they’ll be able to click a temporary threads icon in the upper right corner of AI Mode to engage in a temporary chat. This essentially works the same as a temporary chat would in the stand-alone Gemini chatbot.

The option could be convenient to use at those times when you start digging deeper into a particular topic via AI Mode and realize that you don’t want the discussion to become a part of your search history because of its personal nature.

Once the temporary thread is started, the session becomes detached from your account, Google says. This means that the responses the AI provides won’t be informed by past preferences, nor will they impact future personalization. That could also be useful for those times when you want to learn about something as more of a one-off, but don’t want Google to start to think of the topic as one of your main interests.

When you close out of the thread, you will return to searching as usual.

The company says the option to engage in temporary chats in AI Mode is rolling out globally on desktop and mobile across all supported languages.

Google Warns North Korean Hackers Are Poisoning AI Tools to Drain Crypto Firms

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home » Crypto Market News » Google Warns North Korean Hackers Are Poisoning AI Tools to Drain Crypto Firms

State-backed hackers are using fake recruiters and tampered Claude Code settings to breach crypto networks.

Cyberattacks against the cryptocurrency industry have crossed a dangerous threshold as threat actors shift from basic AI prompts to autonomous AI agents capable of running intrusions from start to finish.

According to the Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) and Mandiant report, state-backed hackers are actively targeting crypto firms in North America and Europe by turning popular AI developer tools into malware delivery traps.

Researchers have linked a suspected North Korean hacking campaign to attacks on cryptocurrency companies. The group, known as Midnight Neptune, uses fake recruiter profiles and tampered software settings to break into company networks.

The attackers approach targets on professional networking platforms, posing as recruiters with AI-generated profile photos.

They then invite victims to fake online meetings, where simulated audio or video problems prompt them to run scripts to fix the issue. This social engineering trick, known as ClickFix, gets victims to execute malicious commands themselves.

Once inside a company’s network, the attackers tamper with its internal code repositories, modifying the configuration file used by Anthropic’s Claude Code.

The next time a developer launches Claude Code, the altered settings silently download a backdoor called SOMBERMEME.

The attackers also create GitHub Personal Access Tokens disguised as Microsoft Copilot to make their activity look like normal developer traffic.

The report also revealed a major advance in automated hacking that puts open-source Web3 infrastructure at risk.

Google researchers discovered an active tool called exploit_agent on an exposed testing server. It uses large language models (LLMs) to compare software code before and after security updates, identify newly patched vulnerabilities, write code to exploit them, and launch attacks — all without human involvement.

That puts open-source protocols at risk. Since code updates are publicly available, hackers can spot newly fixed vulnerabilities and exploit them before operators have a chance to install the patches. Even a brief delay can leave networks vulnerable.

Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) reported that disclosed vulnerabilities and exploits nearly doubled between January and August 2026.

Exploited zero-day vulnerabilities also rose from an average of eight per month in 2025 to 11 in 2026, reaching 22 in August alone.

Beyond traditional cyberattacks, Mandiant warns of a growing threat called LLMJacking. Criminals hijack corporate cloud accounts, steal AI computing power, and resell it through underground marketplaces known as LLM Mixers. The resulting fraud can exceed $100,000 in a single day and reach $1 million per victim.

Meanwhile, influence networks use more than 100,000 fake social media accounts to spread misinformation, fuel token hype, and trigger artificial market panic.

To counter these threats, Google is deploying AI security tools such as Big Sleep and CodeMender to find and fix software vulnerabilities before hackers exploit them.

The GTIG report highlights several security risks for the crypto industry.

Companies using AI coding tools should check their code repositories for unauthorized commands and keep third-party access tokens secure.

They also need to install software updates quickly, as AI-powered hackers can turn newly discovered vulnerabilities into working attacks.

Employees should never follow unexpected browser or video-call instructions asking them to copy and run computer commands.

Investors, meanwhile, should be cautious of sudden waves of online hype or panic, which fake social media accounts can create at scale.
