Latest findings point to fuel supply, not Blue Origin’s engines, in loss of Amazon Leo satellites

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 18, 2026 at 10:01 amNovember 18, 2026 at 10:08 am

Blue Origin’s engines look like they’re off the hook, at least for now.

The two BE-4 engines Blue Origin makes for United Launch Alliance’s Vulcan rocket weren’t to blame for the low thrust in Sunday’s failed launch, according to preliminary findings released Wednesday. Instead, it appears the engines didn’t get enough fuel from the rocket, ULA said.

In its latest update on the investigation, ULA said it had completed an initial integrated review of flight data. Preliminary data indicate the first stage’s low thrust came from “inadequate fuel supply from the vehicle to the BE-4 engines, not from the performance of the engines,” ULA said, adding that work remains to determine the cause.

On Tuesday, ULA said early data suggested the first stage underperformed just after liftoff, leaving the rocket too low and too slow to put 40 of Amazon’s Leo internet satellites into orbit. It didn’t say then whether the engines played a role.

The BE-4s are made by Jeff Bezos’ Kent, Wash.-based Blue Origin. Blue Origin CEO Dave Limp said Wednesday on X that he was proud of the BE-4 team for its “rapid response and support of ULA’s investigation” and that the company looks forward to Vulcan’s return to flight.

The finding matters for Blue Origin beyond Vulcan. Its own New Glenn rocket uses seven BE-4s on its first stage, and Amazon has 24 New Glenn launches reserved for Leo.

ULA repeated that Vulcan’s second stage appears to have started normally and that its RL10 engines operated as planned but couldn’t make up for the earlier loss of performance. The six Northrop Grumman solid rocket boosters, the source of problems on two earlier Vulcan flights, appear to have performed normally, ULA said.

The Federal Aviation Administration is overseeing the ULA-led investigation. Amazon’s next Leo launch is an Ariane 6 mission carrying 36 satellites, targeted for no earlier than Nov. 23 from French Guiana.

ULA said it will share more as the investigation continues.

Grand Theft Auto 6 Performance Is Mostly Stable, Except On Xbox Series S Where It Can Hit Sub-20FPS

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

With Grand Theft Auto 6âs world being as big as it is and current consoles being six years old, thereâs naturally a bit of worry about how the game performs on PS5 and Xbox Series X/S. Our reviewer played the game on a PS5 Pro, considered to be the most powerful console on the market right now, and said that while it runs at 30 frames-per-second most of the time, there were still some noticeable frame drops in crowded areas.

But if you havenât bought a PS5 Pro, what can you expect from GTA 6âs performance? Well, the folks over at Digital Foundry have done what they do best and created some lengthy technical breakdowns of the game, including stress testing it in explosive situations.

According to their video, the PS5 Pro tends to hold a pretty solid 30fps, unless you go around making a mess by blowing up a lot of cars at once; in those moments Digital Foundry saw the game dip to around 24fps at its lowest. The base PS5, however, seems to struggle to maintain 30fps in the best of cases, and if you decide to cause a lot of on-screen destruction, those dips are actually sustained until you leave an area.Â

On the Xbox side, the Xbox Series X seems to share the same relative stability as the PS5 Pro, with the same frame drops during car chases, crowded scenes, or firefights when things get busier on screen. The big question is how the Xbox Series S, which was originally released in 2020 as a cheaper, lower-powered alternative to the bougie, more expensive consoles of the year, handles GTA 6. According to Digital Foundry, there are some details that are less prevalent or rendered with lower-res textures, and the resolution is lower, coming in at 720p compared to the 1440p of Series X.

The DF video has a number of comparison shots demonstrating how a lot of small details have been downgraded on Series S, but the real difference is that the framerate is, unsurprisingly, struggling the most on the less powerful Xbox. Digital Foundry reports that while 30fps is the target, even the most “typical” play can affect framerate, with standard combat encounters and car chases dropping it to around 25 and the more demanding carnage bringing it down to 20. There’s even a few seconds in the video where it gets down to 18 and 19fps during an explosive shootout.

Ultimately, if youâre hoping for the smoothest possible experience, the PS5 Pro and Xbox Series X are the unsurprising winners here. A PC version of the game is rumored to launch sometime next year, and I imagine thatâs where folks with high-end builds are going to be able to get smoother, 60fps framerates as Rockstar apparently has no plans to add a Performance Mode to GTA6 on consoles. If youâre running a Series S, maybe try not to cause too much mayhem along the way for best results.Â

Though the Xbox Series S provided a more affordable way for people to jump into the next generation of gaming consoles in 2020, as time has gone on and games have gotten more demanding on the hardware, developers have expressed frustration with having to make their games compatible with the device. Tekken director Katsuhiro Harada described it as akin to âhaving to do extra work for a slightly weaker child.â

How I’m Approaching AI Search (And How You Should To)

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Learn how to prioritize page fixes, check agent access and measure readiness for your 2027 SEO plan.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Forget citation hacks and obsessing over prompts. AI search is an opportunity to rethink how we approach marketing, audiences and discoverability.

AI is strangely simple. And we’re overcomplicating it.

In focusing on citation tactics and cheap visibility tricks, we’re missing the bigger picture. As an industry, we’ve got away with poor content for years. Updating the same “best spoons” article three times a week because some other idiot is doing exactly the same thing.

There’s nothing inherently wrong with it. It’s just too SEO-y. We’re missing a much bigger opportunity by framing it through a really narrow lens.

I don’t have the energy or the wherewithal to play catch-up every year or two. So I think of AI search as the ultimate synthesis of your marketing efforts.

Your positioning, reputation, expertise, content, and presence across the internet are all potential inputs into how AI systems understand and represent you.

It’s not just about your website anymore.

I’ve spent a lot of time recently working on AI search, information retrieval, audience research, and discoverability strategies. The more I do, the more convinced I become that we need to approach this as marketers, not SEOs.

But don’t build your entire “marketing” strategy around it. I use marketing in the loosest possible sense of the word.

If you’re in dire need of a refresher, here’s a few parts on the fundamentals of modern information retrieval:

There’s one other concept I haven’t written about in detail: query fan-out. Not because it’s not important, more just because a) it’s pretty dull and b) it isn’t a new concept for SEOs. We’ve been using tools like AlsoAsked for years to understand related questions, adjacent information needs and the wider context surrounding a search.

But we also need to distinguish between three things:

They’re related, but they aren’t interchangeable. Being crawled doesn’t guarantee inclusion in training data. Being retrieved doesn’t guarantee a citation. And a citation doesn’t guarantee commercial value.

Understanding these mechanisms is important – mainly just so you can answer the brain-dead questions people ask you about AI. Building your entire marketing strategy around their current implementation is the action of a halfwit.

Models, retrieval systems, and interfaces will change. An owned audience is a considerably more durable investment. So build one.

This is the biggest change I’ve made to my approach. Not just for AI search, but for traditional SEO.

I don’t begin by asking which prompts a brand should appear in. Or how much search volume a keyword or even topic has. Mainly because my trust in search volume and all forms of visibility is at rock bottom.

So I use SparkToro to investigate where audiences spend time, which websites they visit, who influences them, and which platforms they’re disproportionately likely to use compared with the wider population.

For this audience, I know that if I want to influence them, LinkedIn is a non-negotiable (Image Credit: Harry Clarkson-Bennett)

I combine that with traditional search research, AI search behaviour and an assessment of the brand’s existing positioning. You don’t need to overcomplicate this – ask a number of different LLMs about insert brand and record the responses.

I want to answer some relatively straightforward questions:

First, our owned platforms. We use what we’ve learned to improve the way we position ourselves, the services we offer, the language we use, and the content we create.

Let’s try to make things under our control as human and well-targeted as possible.

Second, our wider marketing strategy. We identify the publications, communities, creators, platforms, and people capable of influencing that audience, and decide how to reach them.

Audience affinity with specific Subreddits, via SparkToro (Image Credit: Harry Clarkson-Bennett)

You need to know this. Not because it might get your brand mentioned as part of the training data. But because this is where you should be marketing.

Made-up prompt data is out. Real, human-led marketing is in. If we want SEO to be taken seriously, this is the proper evolution.

The goal isn’t to manufacture mentions for an AI model. It’s to build a brand people want to discover, engage with, and recommend.

If those activities also increase your representation in AI systems, wonderful. But that’s a delicious byproduct, not the reason for doing them.

While audience research informs the wider strategy, your website needs to be accessible, understandable, and representative of who you are. That is something reasonably competent SEOs can lead.

Can the relevant search and AI crawlers access your content? Can they render it? Are you inadvertently blocking systems you want to reach?

This means reviewing bot management, robots directives, rendering, indexability, internal linking, site architecture, and the usual technical SEO fundamentals.

A traditional SEO-type audit with a few improvements.

One that understands that not all AI bots serve the same purpose. Training crawlers, search crawlers, and user-triggered retrieval systems can have different access requirements and commercial implications.

Crawlers do different things (Image Credit: Harry Clarkson-Bennett)

Especially important if you’re working for – or with – a publisher. Someone whose business may depend on you understanding the wider context.

Most large publishers block AI crawlers, sometimes almost indiscriminately, under the banner of copyright protection. There may be very good commercial reasons for doing so. But those decisions fundamentally change your discoverability strategy.

If you’re restricting access to your content, you need to understand what you’re giving up, what you’re protecting, and where the value might exist instead.

Perhaps that’s content licensing, direct audience relationships or proprietary products.

The role of the SEO changes considerably in an environment where you’re not maximising access. You’re helping the business understand the trade-offs between visibility, intellectual property and commercial value.

It’s like a paywall. You’re restricting reach for commercial purposes. It’s the correct move if played properly.

See Cyrus Shepherd’s excellent test to understand how much search traffic was truly influenced by Google’s AI proposition.

This is where I think modern information retrieval methods and SEO intersect perfectly. I’ve written about how to approach this in Entities Are the Foundation of Modern SEO.

This isn’t about adding some structured data and hoping ChatGPT tries to hump your leg. Spoiler: It won’t.

It’s about making sure that your owned presence accurately represents your positioning and that the wider internet provides credible, independent evidence to support it.

So, we know who we are. We’ve represented ourselves properly. Now we have to rely on *shudders* other people to corroborate this message.

This references a podcast I did with Andy Francos and an article I wrote for Lumar two years ago (Image Credit: Harry Clarkson-Bennett)

We’re talking actual, real marketing (not SEO). PR, partnerships, content and audience development.

If the people you’re trying to reach spend their time reading particular publications, following particular experts or participating in particular communities, those are the places worth investing in.

Bollocks to a citation. Your audience is there. You know, people you could actually sell something to.

That might mean publishing original research, collaborating with influential people, appearing on podcasts, creating genuinely useful resources or contributing expertise to relevant publications.

Some of those activities will generate links. Others will generate mentions, relationships, conversations and brand demand – a real representation of site quality in Google.

The more consistently you’re associated with the right subjects, people and organizations, the stronger your wider digital presence can become. That is, in essence, how training data and entity relationships work.

If you’re lucky, some of that evidence may eventually influence how AI systems represent you.

But you won’t care, because you’ll have been making sales by marketing to actual customers.

Modern methods of information retrieval across SEO and AI search share many, if not all of the same fundamentals.

Arguably, AI search has a longer tail to it. But it’s not sustainable, nor sensible, to create a page for every possible thought your audience may have. To perpetuate a false Henry Ford quote:

“If I had asked people what they wanted, they would have said faster horses.” H. Ford, Never

So you need to strike a balance. No one cares about visibility without impact. At least not for long.

There is room to try and test things. Good things. Things real people will care about.

People like Jan-Willem Bobbink and Dan Petrovic highlight some really brilliant things you can test that are eminently sensible for SEO and AI search. Surprise, surprise, they work for people too. It’s marketing.

Let me give you an example. Maybe more accurately, let me steal one of Jan’s:

“I spent the last three weeks on SEO / GEO work for B2B clients, and the changes that made the biggest difference were mostly about information that was missing or hard to find. Buyers run into the same problems on these sites, so you can usually get these fixes approved without having to explain SEO to anyone.”

Being explicit, consistent, and accurate is not an AI hack. It’s just pretty sensible.

Adding pricing pages, case studies, and telling people why you are the best in the business are always good ideas. Even if your pricing pages just say something like prices starting at x, that is a better way of finding the right audience.

And if you want to feature in AI responses, for god’s sake don’t hide your case studies. You need to provide a clear reason for your inclusion. Hard if your best work is hidden.

Statements like “Client Y saw a 30% improvement in deliverability metrics” can noticeably improve your citation rate because a model is literally looking for a reason to include you. You need to give them one. Just as you would a person.

Experience. Expertise. Authority. Trust. Not so stupid after all.

Measurement’s been broken for a decade anyway. It’s just nice for the C-suite to have numbers to report on – even if they’re complete hokum.

You try and figure this out (Image Credit: Harry Clarkson-Bennett)

AI visibility’s not much better. A hugely flawed metric given the personalization, lack of prompt data, and completely individual prompts. But it’s better than nothing. Indicative at best, but you need to use something.

People still want to know how they’re represented in AI systems. And so they should – it’s a valuable channel. My preference is to establish a representative set of prompts across four areas:

Track these consistently across the systems that matter to your audience.

Look at whether you’re mentioned, how you’re described, which competitors appear, and which sources are being used. Find out when you start to appear for certain terms and see if it matches your positioning.

I always track my mentions with Alertmouse (Image Credit: Harry Clarkson-Bennett)

I appear in AI recommendations around news SEO expertise despite having done nothing specifically to optimize for AI search.

What I have done is spend years working in the industry, publishing, building relationships, contributing expertise, and establishing a reasonably clear on-site and off-site identity.

Not a bad list, although I am not sure ChatGPT is quite nailing the profile pictures for us… (Image Credit: Harry Clarkson-Bennett)

I guarantee you, if you ask Barry Adams, he’ll tell you he’s done nothing special for AI either.

I’ve written what I think are high-quality articles on third-party websites and podcasts, featured in roundups, and built my own distribution platform. It just also happens to work for featuring in AI systems.

AI systems don’t necessarily need to send you traffic to influence commercial outcomes. Which is a good thing, because ChatGPT sends less than 1% of Google’s traffic.

Similarweb’s research found that users who received a brand recommendation in ChatGPT were 2.5 times more likely to visit that brand’s website within the following seven days than users who were recommended a competitor.

Much of that activity happened through subsequent branded searches rather than direct AI referrals. One of those measures of site quality that should be monitored.

AI search can and does function as a brand discovery and consideration channel. I think referral traffic is, at best, a secondary measure of success from LLMs. While they’ve engineered the exit out, you cannot deny the reach these systems have.

Unfortunately, our attribution systems can’t cope with “I saw it here once.” We need to focus on incremental change. The outcomes attributable to marketing.

Establish a baseline. Run a campaign. Measure changes in branded demand, direct visits, conversions, leads, and commercial outcomes. Compare with appropriate benchmarks or control groups where possible.

Keep tracking AI visibility, referrals and citations, but use them as supporting indicators.

Our objective is to establish whether said marketing activity is creating additional business value, not whether ChatGPT has linked to your homepage.

For businesses whose models depend on monetizing information and attracting a click, AI search presents a very different challenge.

They are monetizing other people’s work for their own ill-gotten gain. That is an irrefutable commercial dilemma. Hence all the protectionism. So we need to establish what that cost is and try to find alternative methods of monetization.

In The Generative AI Report Sucks – Let’s Make It Brilliant, I outlined how publishers can combine AI visibility data with traffic dependency, commercial value, audience resilience, and server logs.

We can go some way toward solving actual business problems.

Always, always, always a good thing to do. Regardless of your job.

This opportunity is no different, especially for organizations (like publishers) with substantial authority across particular subjects. They have influence within the wider information ecosystem.

That influence can have commercial value.

Publishers Should Think About AI Visibility. Content licensing, data partnerships, research collaborations, and other commercial arrangements offer opportunities to monetize expertise and proprietary information.

Selling links or undisclosed editorial influence is not a sustainable AI strategy. It introduces reputational, editorial, and search-policy risks.

The more sustainable opportunity is to understand the value of your existing content, expertise, audience, and distribution – and build legitimate commercial products around those assets.

Lots of companies are selling or actively considering selling their archived assets or data to AI companies:

For publishers in particular, this is an opportunity to rethink the value they create beyond the click. See the Daily Mail’s approach to GEO monetization here.

If you’re more on the glass half full side, you’ll be able to sniff out commercial opportunities. Ideally ones that don’t ruin the brand or fall foul of Google’s link payment guidelines.

General advice: If something smells a bit off, it probably is.

If you’re a business looking to take this seriously, there’s an organizational question you need to answer.

Because as great as we can be, it shouldn’t just be your SEO.

This touches technical infrastructure, audience research, brand positioning, content, PR, distribution, commercial partnerships, and measurement. That requires someone who understands the entire ecosystem and, crucially, how to influence people.

Nobody is an expert at all of these things. They’re too different. Too complex.

For larger businesses, I’d be thinking about a Head of Marketing or Head of Discoverability with responsibility for coordinating these activities. Someone who at least knows the basics of each and can herd a clowder of cats.

They need the authority to bring different teams together, allocate resources, challenge existing assumptions and measure outcomes against business objectives.

There’s a reason SEOs rarely graduate to marketing lead positions. We don’t understand wider marketing because we’ve rarely had to. SEO was always the best channel… According to wonky data, anyway.

AI search gives us a very good reason to change that.

And for anyone working in SEO, I think there are three particularly interesting directions.

All three have tremendous value. Limiting yourself to SEO, particularly as a collection of search-engine or LLM-specific tactics, is increasingly short-sighted.

Discoverability has been changing for a decade. We need to think much more broadly about how audiences discover and engage with content. The opportunity is to bring those disciplines together.

A little rebrand and refocus on what you want from your career may be the best thing you ever do.

Stop trying to engineer your way into an AI response. Build a brand worth recommending, an audience worth owning, and a business that benefits whether the citation comes or not.

Enjoy yourself. This will be a better era for marketers and marketing. Your competition is lazy and inept. They’re just faster.

SEO Director at The Telegraph with a decade of experience. Unskilled jiu-jitsu guy. Average chess player. Jack of many things.

ULA traces Vulcan Centaur rocket failure to 'inadequate fuel supply' to 1st-stage engines

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The root cause of the anomaly is still under investigation.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

United Launch Alliance (ULA) is getting a handle on why its Vulcan Centaur rocket failed over the weekend.

That failure, which occurred during a launch from Florida's Space Coast on Sunday (Nov. 15), led to the loss of 40 Amazon Leo internet satellites. ULA soon traced the anomaly to Vulcan Centaur's first stage, and it has now pinpointed the problem area even further.

"While work remains to determine cause, preliminary indications are that the low booster thrust was the result of inadequate fuel supply from the vehicle to the BE-4 engines, not from the performance of the engines," ULA wrote in an update today (Nov. 18).

This is good news for Blue Origin, Jeff Bezos' aerospace company, which builds the BE-4. Two of these engines power Vulcan's first-stage booster, and they're also key to Blue Origin's New Glenn rocket: That heavy lifter features seven or nine BE-4s in its first stage, depending on the configuration. (New Glenn has its own issues at the moment; it exploded during a test on May 28, and Blue Origin is working to return the powerful rocket to flight.)

ULA had already said that Vulcan's six solid rocket boosters (SRBs) and its Centaur V upper stage — which employs a different engine, the RL-10 — seem to perform well on Sunday. Further analysis is firming up that conclusion, according to today's update.

Vulcan has had issues with its SRBs in the past. On the rocket's second and fourth flights — which launched in October 2024 and February of this year, respectively — an SRB nozzle burned through and fell off about 30 seconds into flight. On both occasions, Vulcan overcame the problem and successfully delivered its payload to orbit.

In the wake of the February flight, ULA and Northrop Grumman, which builds the SRBs, tweaked the nozzle design — a fix that apparently worked, based on Sunday's nominal performance.

Sunday's launch was the fifth ever for the two-stage Vulcan Centaur, the successor to ULA's workhorse Atlas V rocket. Vulcan debuted in January 2024 and has now experienced issues on three of its five flights, though Sunday's was the only one that ended in failure.

Vulcan was out of action for about nine months after the February SRB issue. It's unclear how long its current grounding will last; that depends on what ULA's anomaly investigation, which the U.S. Federal Aviation Administration is overseeing, ends up finding.

He primarily covers human and robotic spaceflight, military space, and exoplanets, but has been known to dabble in the space art beat. His book about the search for alien life, "Out There," was published on Nov. 13, 2018. Before becoming a science writer, Michael worked as a herpetologist and wildlife biologist. He has a Ph.D. in evolutionary biology from the University of Sydney, Australia, a bachelor's degree from the University of Arizona, and a graduate certificate in science writing from the University of California, Santa Cruz. The former Toffees midfielder reflects on how his old boss understood the fabric of Liverpool - and why his Everton legacy goes far beyond silverware.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Former Everton midfielder Leon Osman has praised David Moyes’ enduring legacy in the blue half of Merseyside, insisting the Scotsman’s Glaswegian grit made him the ideal fit the club

Moyes transformed Everton’s fortunes during an 11-year spell on Merseyside between 2002 and 2013, consistently guiding the Toffees into the top half of the Premier League despite operating on a fraction of the transfer budgets enjoyed by their top-flight rivals.

While Moyes never lifted a major trophy during his tenure - falling just short in the 2009 FA Cup final against Chelsea - Osman believes his former boss’s ability to punch above his weight cements his status as one of the club’s most vital modern figures.

"For the majority of David Moyes' time in charge of Everton, he's probably overachieved in terms of what he's had to spend in the transfer market, so while he might not have had the success Harry Catterick did in terms of silverware, he's been so important for the club," Osman told Betway.

"He's a great fit for Everton, and I think it's that Glaswegian way that adapts particularly well to Liverpool because there's a real understanding of what the people of the city are like and what they want to see from their football team. Everton is the people's club first and foremost, and the fans want to see the team work hard and show that desire to be successful and that's something David Moyes has always instilled in his teams."

Osman was a central pillar of that Moyes era, making over 400 appearances for his boyhood club after breaking into the senior squad under the manager’s guidance. However, the former England international admitted he initially faced an uphill battle to prove he possessed the physicality required for top-flight football before becoming one of Moyes' most trusted lieutenants.

"He's the man who gave me my shot in the Premier League, so I'll always be thankful to him for that," Osman added. "It did take me a while, though, to convince him I was able and I was ready to play at that level, but once he gave me that chance I think he realised that we had very similar outlooks in terms of style of play and how the game should be played and we had a really great relationship throughout my career."

GTA 6 studio head: "Multiple characters was a leap of faith" and Rockstar knew it "might backfire"

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

From the creators of Edge: A free weekly videogame industry newsletter, featuring in-depth news, expert analysis and exclusive interviews

GTA 5's iconic trio had something to prove

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

GTA 5's three switchable characters were decided on "right at the start" of development, but Rockstar always knew that the idea "might backfire."

Speaking to Edge Magazine back in 2013, Aaron Garbut - then art director on GTA 5 and now co-studio head supervising GTA 6 - said that Rockstar new from the beginning that "we wanted to do three switchable characters." But even once the studio had established those characters and the tone of their game, Garbut said that "the initial vision" for the game remained "very rough."

Eventually, Rockstar "just built and evolved and let the game dictate itself," but Garbut acknowledged that "[having] multiple characters was a leap of faith. It was an interesting idea, and it felt like we could do interesting things with it, but it also felt like a change to the core of the game that might backfire." Thankfully, the trio of Michael, Trevor, and Franklin "really worked out" - but Garbut admitted he wasn't sure that would be the case until after that game was out.

"It's so hard during development to get perspective," he said. "You get so used to looking for the problems that it becomes almost impossible to step back and see the positives. On this project, despite living in that world for years, playing the missions again and again, it felt fun. It felt great to experience the world, to see it evolve from the basic building blocks to the finished detailed results with all the scenarios and life added."

I can definitely imagine that in the run-up to GTA 5, the trio of protagonists might have amde for a few sleepless nights at Rockstar. Firstly, the studio was bucking its own trend - of a single protagonist surrounded by a large and zany cast of supporting characters. Second, it was stretching an enormous story between those three, asking the audience to go along with a constantly-changing narrative perspective. Third, and potentially most importantly, it was really challenging the hardware at the time - GTA 5 launched on PS3, remember, and the ability to seamlessly swap across a bustling open world was something that had never been tried before.

By contrast, the duo of Jason and Lucia seems like a dramatically easier sell. For one thing, Rockstar already knows it can achieve this particular feat, and doing it with just two characters definitely seems easier than doing it with three. For another, the hardware should be more comfortable with the challenge. And that's before we even touch on the fact that this is no longer a unique narrative concept anymore - GTA 5 might firmly established it, and then Marvel's Spider-Man 2 helped secure it as a reliable and effective tool for both storytelling and bringing an open world to life.

The Garmin Venu 4 is $449 (down from $549). If you're looking for something not so rugged or sport-specific, the Venu 4 is one of Garmin's best daily health options. It has an AMOLED touchscreen, stainless steel bezel, Gorilla Glass 3 face, and a speaker and microphone that allow it to take voice calls if your phone is within range. It is still a great sports watch, coming with training status, training readiness, and Garmin Coach.

Follow our Black Friday coverage for the latest and best deals our team finds. Another good tip is to only buy what you need. It sounds simple, but making a list of the things you want before jumping into a sale with millions of products helps you focus and make smarter decisions.

The best thing you can do is compare the prices with other retailers that have Black Friday sales. If that seems like too much work, you can just follow our Black Friday coverage (we're already doing all the searching and vetting for you). Some other tips are making sure you're not being sold an older or cheaper version and always reading reviews.

Yes, Garmin is one of the best brands when it comes to rugged sports watches and fitness trackers. The Forerunner line, especially, is a go-to pick for accurate and reliable GPS tracking for runners.

Round Up: GTA 6 Is Not Just the Most Acclaimed Game of 2026, It's the Highest-Rated Game of All Time

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The initial reviews have arrived for GTA 6, and it's safe to say the game has been an absolute smash hit with critics.

The metascore has settled on a 99, making it one point off a "perfect" score of 100. In a year stuffed with fantastic games big and small, it's become the highest rated title of 2026.

More than that, though, it's the highest rated game of all time.

Review GTA 6 (PS5) - The Greatest Open World Game Ever Made

Reaching 99 is almost unheard of; only one other game has achieved the same level of acclaim — The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time (1998).

However, GTA 6 has held onto its 99 score after more than 160 reviews; Ocarina of Time's metascore counts just 22 — though that's of course indicative of the era in which it released. There are simply more gaming outlets now, but that just means it's tougher to get consistent scores.

Rockstar's crime caper has received insanely consistent acclaim, and that might just give it an edge over the Nintendo classic. It's incredibly rare to see near-total agreement on a subjective work like this.

Critics are unanimous in their praise of GTA 6's open world, the relationship between protagonists Jason and Lucia, and the complex systems behind its myriad characters, activities, and missions.

Here are some snippets from just a few of the reviews — including our own, of course.

"GTA 6 is the deepest, densest, most accomplished open world since Red Dead Redemption 2. Rockstar has set a new sandbox standard here, with a world that feels alive and populated with real people; Leonida is a place where it appears almost anything can happen at any time."

"Somehow, GTA 6 lives up to the hype. Rockstar Games has crafted the most incredible open world I've ever experienced in a video game, packed with so much detail that I'll want to continue exploring it for months and likely years to come. It's coupled with a campaign that's full of extremely entertaining missions, led by two main characters who I've become much more invested in than I'd anticipated."

"Like all videogame protagonists, Jason and Lucia are ciphers through which the player exerts their will on the digital world. And yet, through the co-authored interplay of the characters Rockstar has written, and the people you personally tune them into becoming, they form one of the great partnerships in entertainment: beautiful and deadly, open to the possibilities of their world and, despite the violence they inflict on those beyond their circle, driven forward not by fear, but by love."

"A game of opposites and awe, minute detail and impossible scale, Rockstar pushes back the horizon of video games once again with GTA 6. An irreducible mix of silliness, intimacy, grand ideas and rock 'n' roll action - and a load more after that."

"Rockstar Games' latest release is a monumental achievement in storytelling, immersion, gameplay, and breadth of content."

"GTA 6 is the highest-fidelity sandbox of its generation. Rockstar has built an open world of truly astonishing scale, scope, and quality. There's a degree of sophistication to everything GTA 6 does, and there's no aspect of the series' gameplay that hasn't been improved over past instalments."

"Only Rockstar commands the time and money necessary to make a game as dedicated to small details. And only Rockstar can execute on the realising such a granular vision unimpeachably. The obsession with the minutia both explains and justifies the 13-year wait. This game is the culmination of everything the developer has previously achieved in the open-world space that pushes the genre to heights that, in all likelihood, no other developer will be able to reach. GTA 6 is an astonishing open-world action game that I’d wager won’t be outdone until Grand Theft Auto 7."

"Exquisitely detailed, endlessly ripe for experimentation, and uncompromisingly engineered, Grand Theft Auto 6 is the single most impressive video game I have ever played."

"Grand Theft Auto 6 is a modern masterpiece. Rockstar's developers have created a remarkable open world that excels in storytelling, gameplay, visuals, music and technology to achieve something truly unmatched. Minor performance issues don’t dull the sparkle of an absolute must-play experience. GTA 6 was more than worth the wait."

Are you excited to hop into GTA 6 after reading all these glowing reviews? Tell us in the comments section below.

Xbox Unveils New Halo Background & Default 'Event' Background For GTA 6

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you've turned on your Xbox console today and been presented with a background for GTA 6 without selecting it, there's a reason for that. Xbox is promoting the game by making a default "event" background for certain people.

You can let it sit there and do its thing if you want (although unfortunately it's not dynamic), or you can turn it off by going to Settings, Personalisation, My Background and then unticking the "show event-themed backgrounds" box.

In addition to this, Xbox has added a brand-new dynamic background for Halo: Campaign Evolved today, and it actually looks really, really cool. I might be rocking this background for a while to be honest!

Those who haven't been keeping track might also notice an Xbox 25 dynamic background that serves to celebrate Xbox's 25th anniversary. You'll find all of these by going to Settings, Personalisation, My Background and then browsing through the Dynamic Backgrounds that are on offer.

Your Business Is Saving Hours With AI. So Why Are Customers Still Waiting?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Before investing in another automation tool, ask whether the time you’re saving is actually improving your customers’ experience.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Recently, while working on a healthcare client’s project, my team encountered a problem that had nothing to do with our ability to do the work.

We needed access to the client’s digital platforms. What should have been a straightforward process became a series of emails, permission issues, authentication problems and confusion over which accounts controlled what.

The actual work wasn’t the obstacle. Getting everything in place so we could do the work was.

I’ve encountered different versions of this problem throughout my consulting career. Teams have the expertise. The technology exists. Everyone wants to move forward. Yet progress stalls because the underlying process is more complicated than anyone realized.

It made me think about how businesses are approaching artificial intelligence. We’re investing in tools that promise to make work faster, but how often do we stop to examine what’s slowing the business down in the first place?

When entrepreneurs think about productivity, they naturally focus on the time employees spend completing tasks. How quickly can we produce a report? How much time can we save creating content? Can AI respond to routine inquiries? Those are reasonable questions.

But consider what happened in the healthcare project. Automating another task wouldn’t have resolved who owned the account, who could authorize access or how the team should handle authentication. Those were process and accountability problems.

This is why I believe every business should examine its workflows before investing heavily in automation.

I’ve previously written about why entrepreneurs should examine workflows before hiring more employees. The same principle applies to technology.

If responsibilities are unclear and information is scattered, automation may simply help a flawed process move faster.

A business owner might celebrate saving 20 hours a week. The customer is asking a different question: Why am I still waiting?

That gap is easy to overlook because internal performance metrics don’t always reflect the customer experience.

Imagine improving the speed of customer service responses while leaving the underlying resolution process unchanged. Customers might receive answers sooner but still wait days for their problems to be addressed.

The business reports an efficiency improvement. Customers experience little difference. I see a similar disconnect in marketing. Companies sometimes invest in generating more leads without examining what happens after those leads arrive.

As I explored in my article about why businesses can spend more on marketing while attracting fewer customers, more activity doesn’t automatically translate into better commercial results. The lesson applies to AI as well.

Measure the outcome, not just the activity that produced it.

One thing I’ve learned from managing complex projects is that technology cannot replace clear ownership. Someone still needs to make decisions, resolve exceptions and take responsibility when something goes wrong.

Before automating a process, I ask three questions:

If those answers aren’t clear, technology isn’t the first thing I would fix.

For example, automated appointment reminders can reduce administrative effort. But if patients cannot easily reschedule or reach someone when they need help, the experience remains frustrating.

The same is true for sales, onboarding and service delivery. Customers appreciate convenience, but they also want confidence that someone is paying attention.

This may be the most important question entrepreneurs should ask about AI.

Saving time is only valuable when businesses put that time to better use. This is the AI productivity trap: Faster work doesn’t necessarily mean faster growth. The real opportunity lies in using that efficiency to improve customer relationships, solve problems and create measurable business value.

Suppose a new system saves your team 10 hours every week. What do you do with that time? Do you reinvest it in customer relationships? Improve service quality? Reduce expenses? Give employees more opportunities to solve problems that technology cannot handle?

Or does that time simply disappear into additional meetings and administrative work? The answer matters.

In my earlier Entrepreneur article about why business growth doesn’t always improve profitability, I discussed the importance of looking beyond top-line results. Productivity deserves the same scrutiny.

An efficient business isn’t necessarily a profitable one, and a productive team isn’t automatically delivering a better customer experience.

Entrepreneurs need to decide how efficiency gains will create value before assuming they will.

My advice is simple: Choose one process that repeatedly creates problems. It might be customer onboarding, appointment scheduling, account access, proposal approvals or resolving support requests. Follow that process from beginning to end.

Talk to the people involved. Identify where information gets lost, approvals stall or responsibilities become unclear. Then ask whether the problem requires automation at all.

Sometimes the answer is a better system. Sometimes it’s eliminating an unnecessary approval. Sometimes it’s giving one person clear responsibility for following through.

Make one change, measure what happens, and expand what works. You may discover that the most valuable improvement costs considerably less than another software subscription.

I’m enthusiastic about what AI can do for entrepreneurs, especially smaller businesses competing with organizations that have significantly greater resources. But I don’t believe adopting more technology automatically makes a company more competitive.

The healthcare project reinforced something I’ve seen repeatedly: Even capable teams with good technology can struggle when the underlying process isn’t working.

Your customers don’t see how many hours you save internally. They notice whether you deliver what you promised, respond when they need you and make their lives easier.

Before celebrating how much faster your business has become, make sure your customers can feel the difference.

Recently, while working on a healthcare client’s project, my team encountered a problem that had nothing to do with our ability to do the work.

We needed access to the client’s digital platforms. What should have been a straightforward process became a series of emails, permission issues, authentication problems and confusion over which accounts controlled what.

The actual work wasn’t the obstacle. Getting everything in place so we could do the work was.

Sources: David Moyes eyeing 'dream' job as Everton exit looms

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Founder & Managing Director AUTHORITY Former chief correspondent at Evening Standard, Goal and Press Association with nearly three decades in national, regional and digital news and sports journalism. Wayne directs the network’s news strategy and high-level operations. FOCUS Editorial direction for the 11-site network and newsroom management. THE BRIEF Wayne oversees the network’s editorial standards across the network. He provides the final sign-off on all headline scoops, ensuring content across all platforms meets the standards required for a high-velocity newsroom.

David Moyes is “very attracted” to becoming the next Scotland manager and it is his “dream last job”, sources have told Football Insider.

The 2026-27 campaign is likely to be Moyes' last at Everton unless their impending takeover changes his circumstances.

American owners The Friedkin Group (TFG) recently announced their intention to seek outside investment or a full sale of the Merseyside club.

Moyes is in the final year of his contract with the club, which expires next June, and talks over a new deal are yet to progress amid uncertainty over the future of their owners.

He is not believed to be on the same page as TFG after feeling like he was left well short of new signings in the summer transfer window.

Sources say the Scotland job is hugely appealing to Moyes and he would like to manage his country before retiring from the dugout.

Moyes could be relieved of his duties by Everton's potential next owners, but they may hand him a new deal if he continues to do an impressive job at the Hill Dickinson Stadium.

He has split opinions among fans since returning to the club, but has the Toffees sat sixth in the Premier League and into the quarter-finals of the Carabao Cup right now.

The 63-year-old will not be short of suitors if he is forced out of Everton, with sources revealing to Football Insider that Celtic are keen on appointing Moyes to succeed Martin O'Neill.

He is looking to return to his home country, but would prefer to manage the national team above a Premiership club.

Sebastien Pocognoli is coming under increasing pressure as Scotland boss after winning just two of his first six games in charge since taking over after the World Cup.

Moyes would like to be in the frame to follow the Belgian manager if he is relieved of his duties in the coming months.

After being asked by talkSPORT co-host Jeff Stelling if he would like to take charge of his country, ex-Scotland star McCoist put Moyes forward as his number one pick to succeed the struggling Pocognoli.

He said: "It's an obvious answer and it ain't going to happen: David Moyes. I think it's a no-brainer. What he's doing at Everton right now is absolutely remarkable.

"Moyes could have the Scotland job tomorrow if he wanted it. And I think one day it might suit him to take it. To be brutally honest with you, Jeff, I think he'd be perfect for it. But not now. He would be my number one choice."
